4227 B7D0 04C0  14 	clr r0
4228 B7D2 D800  38 	movb r0,@cvb_KJF
     B7D4 208A     
4229               	; 	kjh = 0
4230 B7D6 04C0  14 	clr r0
4231 B7D8 D800  38 	movb r0,@cvb_KJH
     B7DA 208C     
4232               	; 	kanim = 0
4233 B7DC 04C0  14 	clr r0
4234 B7DE D800  38 	movb r0,@cvb_KANIM
     B7E0 20F8     
4235               	;
4236               	; 	' HARRY STARTS ON FLOOR 2 AT THE WEST EDGE OF SCREEN 7, and that is as
4237               	; 	' far left as the clock allows. The research says "the second-floor
4238               	; 	' elevator door", which is screen 3, and it cannot be done:
4239               	; 	'
4240               	; 	'   spawn      escape     round
4241               	; 	'   screen 3   119.9 s    100 s   -- impossible
4242               	; 	'   screen 5   108.1 s    100 s   -- impossible
4243               	; 	'   screen 6   102.3 s    100 s   -- impossible
4244               	; 	'   screen 7    96.4 s    100 s   -- 3.6 s of room
4245               	; 	'
4246               	; 	' HE STARTS AT THE LIFT NOW, AND THAT COST HIM A SPEED CHANGE.
4247               	; 	'
4248               	; 	' His escape route ZIGZAGS -- east along floor 2 to its escalator, WEST
4249               	; 	' along floor 3 to that one, then east again along the roof to the door.
4250               	; 	' From the lift on screen 3 that is 5,120 px against the 4,208 he walked
4251               	; 	' from screen 7, so at his old 1.75 px a pass he needed 105 s of a 100 s
4252               	; 	' round: he could never escape at all, and one of the two loss conditions
4253               	; 	' would quietly have stopped existing. This block used to say exactly that
4254               	; 	' and conclude the lift was impossible. It was impossible AT THAT SPEED.
4255               	; 	'
4256               	; 	' AND QUARTER-PIXELS COULD NOT EXPRESS THE ANSWER. A quarter of a pixel
4257               	; 	' per pass is worth about TEN SECONDS of his route, so the two candidate
4258               	; 	' values straddled the buzzer with nothing in between:
4259               	; 	'
4260               	; 	'   2.00 px/pass   escapes at 102.4 s of a 100 s round   never escapes
4261               	; 	'   2.25 px/pass   escapes at  91.0 s                    nine seconds early
4262               	; 	'
4263               	; 	' Worse, 2.25 was not reachable at all: the accumulator below had two
4264               	; 	' drain steps and therefore a hard ceiling of 2 px a pass, so hsp4 = 9
4265               	; 	' silently delivered 2.00 and he fell two thirds of a screen short of his
4266               	; 	' own escape. See the accumulator comment for the full account.
4267               	; 	'
4268               	; 	' Sixteenths with three drains put the step at about 2.5 s instead of 10,
4269               	; 	' and hsp64 = 35 (2.1875 px/pass) lands him at 96.3 s of the 100 --
4270               	; 	' escaping as the timer expires, which is what was asked for.
4271               	; 	'
4272               	; 	' 35 AND NOT 34, AND THE DIFFERENCE IS THE PLAYER'S OWN SCREEN. Measured
4273               	; 	' in play at 34: standing on an ordinary screen he escaped with one timer
4274               	; 	' unit left, and standing on the ELEVATOR screen he ran out of clock --
4275               	; 	' same route, same constant, decided by where the player happened to be.
4276               	; 	' Everything that moves is paced per loop PASS while the clock is paced in
4277               	; 	' real FRAMES, so a busy screen slows Harry in real time and the clock does
4278               	; 	' not slow with him. A whole round measured 2,335 passes over about 98 s --
4279               	; 	' 23.8 a second, not the 25 the model assumes -- and the heavy screens are
4280               	; 	' slower again. 35 buys the margin that covers the worst of them. The
4281               	; 	' underlying split is recorded in DESIGN.md 0f-ter; this constant only
4282               	; 	' papers over it.
4283               	; 	'
4284               	; 	' The margin
4285               	; 	' that remains is the ROUND's, not the chase's: Kelly still has to get
4286               	; 	' there first, and does. assets/checkchase.py walks both routes out of
4287               	; 	' these constants, models the drain ceiling, and fails the build if the

4452               	;
4453               	; 	' HARRY'S SPEED IS IN QUARTER PIXELS, AND IT DOES NOT RAMP. It used to be
4454               	; 	' 2 px/frame against Kelly's 3, which sounds like a comfortable 1.5x --
4455               	; 	' and Kelly still lost the race to the roof by ELEVEN SECONDS, every
4456               	; 	' round, because the two routes are not the same length. Kelly runs
4457               	; 	' 7,992 px (three traverses plus the roof); Harry runs 4,104, because he
4458               	; 	' spawns beside floor 2's escalator and so skips a traverse. A chase
4459               	; 	' resolves on PATH / SPEED, and Kelly needs to be more than TWICE
4460               	; 	' Harry's speed before he is ahead at all. See DESIGN.md 4a.
4461               	; 	'
4462               	; 	' 1.75, AND THE NUMBER IS MEASURED, NOT GUESSED. Tracked off the
4463               	; 	' reference video (DESIGN.md 4a), the 2600's Kelly covers 0.40 screen
4464               	; 	' widths a second and its Harry 0.193 -- a ratio of 2.07. At 1.5 ours
4465               	; 	' was 2.67 and the crook visibly strolled. 1.75 brings it to 2.29.
4466               	; 	'
4467               	; 	' 2.0 would match the original exactly and still cannot be used, because
4468               	; 	' OUR routes are not the original's: Kelly's is 1.95x Harry's here, so a
4469               	; 	' speed ratio of 2.0 leaves him 5.2 s ahead at the escape edge -- less
4470               	; 	' than one beach ball (9 s). At 1.75 he is 10.1 s ahead, which absorbs a
4471               	; 	' hit. That figure counts the LIFT, which is on his way and carries him
4472               	; 	' two floors; the foot-only route is 5 s worse and is not the route
4473               	; 	' anybody takes.
4474               	; 	'
4475               	; 	' THE PER-KROOK DIAL IS STILL NOT HIS SPEED. It is the obstacles, which
4476               	; 	' is also the only dial the manual names (`obsp` above, and the ball
4477               	; 	' arcs). assets/checkchase.py checks all of this mechanically -- run it
4478               	; 	' if you touch these numbers.
4479               	; 	hsp64 = 57			' 0.891 px per FRAME, every Krook

4505 B96E 0450  20 	b *r0
4506               	;
4507               	; 	' ======================================================================
4508               	; 	' DRAWING THE STORE -- four blits, once per screen crossing
4509               	; 	' ======================================================================
4510               	; 	' This is the whole cost of the flip, and it is paid on a discrete event
4511               	; 	' rather than every frame. RallyX's pan repaints 576 chars every time the
4512               	; 	' camera moves one cell; this repaints 640 about once every two seconds
4513               	; 	' of running.
4514               	; 	' SPRITES OFF BEFORE THE BLIT, NOT AFTER IT. This routine WAITs between
4515               	; 	' bands -- one band a frame, because a burst of VDP writes past a few
4516               	; 	' dozen in one frame is silently dropped -- so painting the new screen
4517               	; 	' takes several frames, and for all of them the OLD screen's actors and
4518               	; 	' obstacles were still sitting on top of it. Following Harry through a
4519               	; 	' seam showed him and every cart from the floor he had just left,
4520               	; 	' standing on a shop that was assembling itself underneath them.
4521               	; 	'
4522               	; 	' They are put back by the normal draw on the same pass, so the crossing
4523               	; 	' reads as a cut rather than as a dissolve with ghosts in it.
4524               	; 	' EACH BAND IS FINISHED BEFORE THE FRAME IT IS DRAWN IN ENDS, and the
4525               	; 	' bands go TOP DOWN. Both halves of that matter.
4526               	; 	'
4527               	; 	' The blit paints the template -- shelves, pillars and all -- and the
4528               	; 	' radios and prizes then take out the fixtures they stand in. Those two
4529               	; 	' used to be four frames apart: all four bands blitted with a WAIT
4530               	; 	' between them, and only afterwards the clearing. So every screen change
4531               	; 	' showed its counters and beams for several frames and then erased them,
4532               	; 	' which reads as the shop flickering rather than as it being drawn.
4533               	; 	'
4534               	; 	' TOP DOWN because a band's beam tops are stamped into the SLAB ROW OF
4535               	; 	' THE BAND ABOVE. Going bottom-up, band 1's blit would paint over the
4536               	; 	' beam tops band 0 had just put there. Going top-down the band above is
4537               	; 	' always already drawn, so everything a band needs -- its own cells and
4538               	; 	' the row it borrows overhead -- is final by the time its WAIT arrives.
4539               	; 	'
4540               	; 	' `3 - dq` rather than `FOR dlv = 3 TO 0 STEP -1`: dlv is an unsigned
4541               	; 	' byte, so counting down past zero wraps to 255 and the loop never ends
4542               	; 	' (CLAUDE.md 3A).
4543               	; draw_screen:
4544               cvb_DRAW_SCREEN
4545               	; 	GOSUB hide_play
4546 B970 06A0  32 	bl @jsr
     B972 A980     
4547 B974 EA9E     	data cvb_HIDE_PLAY

4549 B976 06A0  32 	bl @jsr

     B980 21A6     
4554               cv135
4555               	; 		' WAIT FIRST, NOT LAST. A band is blitted and then corrected -- the
4556               	; 		' beam top stamped on, the pillar under a radio taken off -- and the
4557               	; 		' raster does not stop while that happens. Starting the burst in the
4558               	; 		' middle of a frame let the raster pass the band between the blit and
4559               	; 		' the correction, so the uncorrected version was shown for a frame:
4560               	; 		' at round start, after CLS, that reads as a support beam appearing
4561               	; 		' and being rubbed out. Beginning at vblank gives the whole burst a
4562               	; 		' clear run at the rows before the beam reaches them. It also still
4563               	; 		' gives each band its own frame, which is what stops a VDP write
4564               	; 		' burst past a few dozen from being silently dropped.
4565               	; 		WAIT

4651 BA5A C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0
4652 BA5C 0450  20 	b *r0
4653               	;
4654               	; 	' A SUPPORT BEAM HAS TO REACH THE FLOOR IT HOLDS UP, AND THAT FLOOR IS
4655               	; 	' NOT IN ITS OWN BAND. A beam fills its band's four air rows, so its top
4656               	; 	' pixel sits under the slab row of the band ABOVE -- and a slab row is
4657               	; 	' five pixels of bar over three of green (see SLAB), so the beam stopped
4658               	; 	' three pixels short of the bar on every storey. It read as a beam that
4659               	; 	' misses the floor, which is exactly what it was.
4660               	; 	'
4661               	; 	' It cannot be drawn into either template. The beam belongs to the band
4662               	; 	' below and the row belongs to the band above, and the two templates are
4663               	; 	' chosen independently per screen, so no template knows both. So the
4664               	; 	' band blits go down first and the tops are stamped afterwards, from a
4665               	; 	' per-template column table -- the same shape as the escalator's head cap.
4666               	; 	'
4667               	; 	' Bands 0 and 1 only. Band 2's top row is the ROOF DECK, which is grey
4668               	; 	' over grey with no green to bridge, and the roof itself has no band
4669               	; 	' above it at all.
4670               	; 	' BANDS 0 TO 2 -- ALL THREE SHOPPING FLOORS. It used to stop at 1,
4671               	; 	' because band 2's ceiling is the ROOF DECK and the deck had no green
4672               	; 	' under it: a beam there already ran straight into grey and needed
4673               	; 	' nothing. Giving the roof its three green rows (so the lift shaft stops
4674               	; 	' under it, like every other floor) took that away, and the top floor's
4675               	; 	' beams started falling three pixels short.
4676               	; 	'
4677               	; 	' So the roof gets its own version of the stamp. A beam HOLDS THE ROOF
4678               	; 	' UP and must reach it; the shaft merely stops beneath it.
4679               	; beam_one:
4680               cvb_BEAM_ONE
4681               	; 	IF bt > 2 THEN RETURN		' the roof has no band above it

5214               	; 			' AND HOW MANY FLOORS CARRY ONE AT ALL. The gates above
5215               	; 			' decide what KIND a hazard is; this decides whether the

5219               	; 			' arrived yet to a beach ball rather than leaving the floor

5221               	; 			' Krook 1 was a ball on all FOUR floors of every populated
5222               	; 			' screen. All four bands are on screen at once, so that is
5223               	; 			' four identical balls in view at all times, which is not
5224               	; 			' what "merely a few beach balls" describes.
5225               	; 			'
5226               	; 			' Per-band count was never the problem: only one slot is

5228               	; 			' and it had no ramp at all.
5229               	; 			'
5230               	; 			' Floors 1 and 3 stay clear on Krook 1, floor 1 joins on

5274               	; 			' NO SPRITE HAZARD ON THE FLOOR THE CROOK IS STANDING ON,
5275               	; 			' AND THE DECISION IS MADE HERE -- once, as the screen is
5276               	; 			' drawn, and never revisited while it is on screen.
5277               	; 			'
5278               	; 			' It was a test in the draw loop and another in the
5279               	; 			' collision test, both re-evaluated every frame, so the
5280               	; 			' hazards came and went as Harry walked on and off the
5281               	; 			' screen: run him to the edge and a floor's worth of balls
5282               	; 			' appeared out of nothing behind him. A rule about what
5283               	; 			' this screen CONTAINS cannot be a per-frame question --
5284               	; 			' the answer changes while the player is looking at it.
5285               	; 			'
5286               	; 			' Zeroing the kind here also makes both of those tests
5287               	; 			' unnecessary: an obstacle that was never loaded cannot be
5288               	; 			' drawn and cannot hit anybody.

5371               	; 			' from one edge, so the obstacles start at the OTHER edge and
5372               	; 			' run toward him. Crossing a seam then always presents an
5373               	; 			' ONCOMING stream, never a set of backs he has to catch up
5374               	; 			' with -- and it means the direction he is travelling is
5375               	; 			' always the direction the danger comes from.
5376               	; 			'
5377               	; 			' The table's x becomes a stagger from that edge rather than
5378               	; 			' an absolute position, and the slot index spreads them out
5379               	; 			' so they arrive in sequence instead of as one wall.
5380               	; 			'
5381               	; 			' THE SECOND ONE IS A FIXED DISTANCE BEHIND THE FIRST, AND
5382               	; 			' THAT DISTANCE IS SET BY THE JUMP.
5383               	; 			'
5384               	; 			' It used to be `(lx AND 63) + 64` -- a masked byte out of
5385               	; 			' the placement table plus a constant -- so the gap between
5386               	; 			' the two hazards was whatever those two bytes happened to
5387               	; 			' differ by. Measured across the five populated screens it
5388               	; 			' came out 46, 46, 70, 70, 70 px. Screens 2 and 4 flank the
5389               	; 			' elevator, so the store had a visibly tighter pair on one
5390               	; 			' side of it than the other, which is exactly how it was
5391               	; 			' reported from play.
5392               	; 			'
5393               	; 			' Both numbers are too small, which is the real fault. Kelly
5394               	; 			' closes on an oncoming hazard at WALKSP + the hazard's own
5395               	; 			' speed = 6 px a pass, and a jump lasts about 30 passes, so
5396               	; 			' a jump eats ~120 px of closing distance. At 46 or 70 there
5397               	; 			' is NO screen where he can land between the two: he clears
5398               	; 			' the first and comes down on the second.
5399               	; 			'
5400               	; 			' HAZGAP is that distance with margin. The first hazard
5401               	; 			' still lands where the table puts it, so the placement
5402               	; 			' variety is unchanged; only the pairing is now a fact about
5403               	; 			' the jump rather than an accident of two bytes.
5404               	; 			' assets/checkspace.py measures it and fails the build.
5405               	; 			' THE SECOND SLOT IS MEASURED FROM THE FIRST, not from its
5406               	; 			' own table byte. Adding HAZGAP to its own `lx AND 63` would
5407               	; 			' still leave the GAP varying by up to 63 px between screens
5408               	; 			' -- the same fault in a politer form, and the reason the two
5409               	; 			' sides of the elevator differed in the first place. Holding
5410               	; 			' slot 0's stagger and adding to that makes the gap exactly
5411               	; 			' HAZGAP on every screen, while slot 0 still lands wherever
5412               	; 			' the table puts it, so the placement variety is untouched.
5413               	; 			stag = lx AND 63

5493 C05A D401  30 	movb r1,*r0
5494               	; 			' THE BALL IS ON THE GROUND WHEN HE GETS TO IT.
5495               	; 			'
5496               	; 			' The bounce phase used to be `lx AND 31` -- a byte out of
5497               	; 			' the placement table, which is to say arbitrary. Run onto
5498               	; 			' a screen and the first ball might be at the bottom of its
5499               	; 			' arc, where a jump clears it, or at the top, where it has
5500               	; 			' to be ducked, and nothing on the way in told you which.
5501               	; 			' Sometimes you could keep running and jump it; sometimes
5502               	; 			' the same approach at the same speed could not be jumped
5503               	; 			' at all. That is not difficulty, it is a coin toss.
5504               	; 			'
5505               	; 			' So the phase is worked BACKWARDS from the meeting. He
5506               	; 			' closes 4 px a pass and the ball 2, so they meet in
5507               	; 			' (gap / 6) passes; the phase advances one a pass and wraps
5508               	; 			' at 32, so seeding it with the NEGATIVE of that lands it on
5509               	; 			' phase 0 -- the ground -- exactly as they arrive.
5510               	; 			'
5511               	; 			' The gap is the same either way round: he enters at one
5512               	; 			' wall and the ball starts `stag` in from the other, so it
5513               	; 			' is 232 - stag whichever direction the floor runs.
5514               	; 			'
5515               	; 			' It stays forgiving rather than exact. The arc is flat near
5516               	; 			' the bottom, so anything within eight passes either side is
5517               	; 			' still under the jump; and it only holds for an unbroken
5518               	; 			' run, which is the case the player is entitled to read off
5519               	; 			' the screen. Duck, stop or take a hit and everything after
5520               	; 			' is out of step again -- which is the game.
5521               	; 			obp(li) = lx AND 31
5522 C05C D020  34 	movb @cvb_LX,r0
     C05E 21DF

     C7A4 C842     
6415               cv306
6416               	; 		' HE IS STANDING ON A STEP, so he moves at the STEP's rate: 2 px
6417               	; 		' across and 1 up PER PASS -- the staircase's own slope, and exactly
6418               	; 		' what one phase of esc_tick moves the pattern by.
6419               	; 		'
6420               	; 		' NOT `fdv`, WHICH IS THE WHOLE POINT. Everything else in this loop
6421               	; 		' is paced by the frame delta, and pacing him that way too made him
6422               	; 		' outrun the steps the moment a pass cost more than one frame: his
6423               	; 		' feet started planted on a tread and ended floating above it.
6424               	; 		' Pacing the ANIMATION by fdv instead is worse -- four phases cannot
6425               	; 		' be stepped by 2 or 3 without aliasing, and the staircase runs
6426               	; 		' backwards (see esc_tick). The only arrangement that cannot drift
6427               	; 		' is the one where both are driven by the same fixed step, so the
6428               	; 		' rider is clocked by the pass, like the steps he is standing on.
6429               	; 		'
6430               	; 		' THE FIRST FEW PASSES ARE THE STEP ON. Walking on, his feet are on
6431               	; 		' the floor and the bottom tread is 4 to 7 px above them, so he
6432               	; 		' climbs at 2 px a pass until he catches it (the tread is rising at
6433               	; 		' 1, so the gap closes at 1 a pass) and then rides with it. Jumping
6434               	; 		' on he lands ON a tread, so eson is 0 and this never runs.
6435               	; 		IF esy < eson THEN esy = esy + 1
6436 C7A6 D020  34 	movb @cvb_ESY,r0

6737               	;
6738               	; 	' --- running
6739               	; 	' THE END WALLS ARE MOVEMENT LIMITS, NOT A CLAMP APPLIED AFTERWARDS.
6740               	; 	'
6741               	; 	' They used to be the latter, and that is the whole bug: on screen 7 Kelly
6742               	; 	' walked on to x = 254, the crossing routine then noticed there was no
6743               	; 	' screen 8 and set him to 232 -- a 22 px snap BACKWARDS. He reached the
6744               	; 	' wall and was thrown off it. Testing the limit BEFORE the step means he
6745               	; 	' arrives at the wall and stays there, which is what a wall does.
6746               	; 	' IN THE AIR HE IS BALLISTIC. The horizontal direction is fixed at
6747               	; 	' take-off and the stick is ignored until he lands: steering in mid-air
6748               	; 	' is not a jump, it is flight, and it also let him flip which way he was
6749               	; 	' facing halfway through an arc. kjdx is 0 for a standing jump, 1 for a
6750               	; 	' running one to the right, 2 to the left.

7066               	; 	' ------------------------------------------------------------ escalator
7067               	; 	' ONE working escalator per floor, at that floor's alternating end --
7068               	; 	' west, east, west. An escalator drawn where none works would be a step
7069               	; 	' the player runs to and nothing happens.
7070               	; 	' ESCALATORS RUN BOTH WAYS. Stand at the foot and you are carried up;
7071               	; 	' stand at the head of the flight that arrives from the floor below and
7072               	; 	' you are carried down. It is one staircase used from either end, not two
7073               	; 	' -- which is also why the down zone on a floor is the UP zone of the
7074               	; 	' floor beneath it, offset by one band.
7075               	; 	' UP ONLY. Riding down was built at one point and has been taken out
7076               	; 	' again at the reviewer's direction -- these only ever climb.
7077               	; 	'
7078               	; 	' Losing it also removed the re-board lock, which existed solely because
7079               	; 	' stepping off at the head put you inside the zone that rode the same
7080               	; 	' flight back down. With no down ride there is no such zone: a floor's
7081               	; 	' escalator is at the opposite end from the one below it, so arriving at

7641               	;
7642               	; 	' THE VICTROLA, AS CHARACTERS. It never moves, so a sprite spent a
7643               	; 	' scan-line slot on something that can be painted once when the screen is
7644               	; 	' drawn -- and at one 16x16 sprite it could only ever be one colour and
7645               	; 	' one size. Four cells is twice the area for no sprite at all.
7646               	; 	'
7647               	; 	' It stands ON the slab, so it fills the band's rows 2 and 3; row 4 is
7648               	; 	' the floor bar itself and draw_prizes uses row 3 for a collectible,
7649               	; 	' which is why the two never share a screen (genstore keeps prizes off
7650               	; 	' the screens that carry hazards).
7651               	; 	'
7652               	; 	' rdall = 1 paints all four cells, 0 repaints only the top-right one --
7653               	; 	' the pulse. Same address arithmetic either way, so the two cannot drift.
7654               	; 	' A BEAM TOP HANGS OUT OF THE FLOOR ABOVE, and the beam it belongs to has
7655               	; 	' just been taken away. `beam_tops` stamps SLABP -- the floor bar with the
7656               	; 	' support carried up through its lower three pixel rows -- into the slab
7657               	; 	' row above every pillar, and it runs BEFORE the radios and prizes are
7658               	; 	' placed. Clearing a pillar's own cells therefore left three grey pixels
7659               	; 	' dangling from the ceiling with nothing under them.
7660               	; 	'
7661               	; 	' TESTED, NOT ASSUMED: only SLABP is put back to SLAB. The row above the
7662               	; 	' top shopping floor is the ROOF DECK, a different character altogether,
7663               	; 	' and writing a floor bar over it would punch a hole in the roof.
7664               	; 	' A SHELF RUN IS FIVE CELLS WIDE and a radio covers two, so one standing
7665               	; 	' on a counter punched a hole through its middle and left the ends either
7666               	; 	' side -- the same incoherence the dangling pillar had, and worse, because
7667               	; 	' a counter reads as one object. The whole unit goes: walk outward along
7668               	; 	' the band's row 2 while the cell is still a shelf top, clearing it and
7669               	; 	' its bottom half together.
7670               	; 	'
7671               	; 	' Bounded at eight steps. The runs are five, so eight is slack rather
7672               	; 	' than a guess -- and an unbounded walk would run off the end of the row
7673               	; 	' if the name table ever held something unexpected.
7674               	; wipe_shelf:
7675               cvb_WIPE_SHELF
7676               	; 	wsn = 8

8344               	; 				' THEY WRAP. They do not turn round at the wall.
8345               	; 				' A bouncing obstacle is a pendulum: it has a near
8346               	; 				' end and a far end, and the player learns to stand
8347               	; 				' at the far one and wait. Wrapping makes the floor
8348               	; 				' a STREAM that keeps arriving from the same side,
8349               	; 				' so standing still is never the answer and the
8350               	; 				' player has to keep moving -- which is the whole
8351               	; 				' shape of the original.
8352               	; 				IF obd(ui) = 0 THEN
8353 D5BE D020  34 	movb @cvb_UI,r0
     D5C0 2209     
8354 D5C2 0980  32 	srl r0,8

     D5C6 226A

     D5CE D626

8907               	;
8908               	; 	' THE SIXTEENTH-PIXEL ACCUMULATOR. hx is one unsigned byte per screen
8909               	; 	' (2), so there is nowhere to keep a fraction -- the fraction lives here
8910               	; 	' and is spent as whole pixels. THREE IFs, not a loop and not a divide:
8911               	; 	' `%` and `/` both compile to a real TMS9900 DIV (CLAUDE.md 3A) and this
8912               	; 	' runs every pass.
8913               	; 	'
8914               	; 	' THE NUMBER OF DRAINS IS A CEILING ON THE SPEED, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE
8915               	; 	' POINT OF THIS COMMENT. Each IF can spend one whole pixel, so N drains
8916               	; 	' cap hspd at N px per pass NO MATTER WHAT hsp64 SAYS. The invariant is:
8917               	; 	'
8918               	; 	'     hacc is under 16 on entry, and hsp64 is at most 48,
8919               	; 	'     so hacc is under 64 here and three drains always suffice.
8920               	; 	'
8921               	; 	' KEEP hsp64 <= 48. This was quarter-pixels with TWO drains and the same
8922               	; 	' invariant written the same way -- "hsp4 is at most 8, so it can never
8923               	; 	' need a third" -- and it was correct until somebody set hsp4 = 9 without
8924               	; 	' re-reading it. The crook then ran at a flat 2.0 px/pass while every
8925               	; 	' comment, the design table and assets/checkchase.py all said 2.25, and
8926               	; 	' the surplus quarter leaked into hacc and wrapped the byte every 256
8927               	; 	' passes. Nothing failed; he simply arrived two thirds of a screen short
8928               	; 	' of his own escape. checkchase.py now counts these IFs and refuses a
8929               	; 	' speed the drains cannot deliver.
8930               	; 	'
8931               	; 	' 7, NOT 6, AND THE REASON IS MEASURED. Tracked off the reference video
8932               	; 	' (DESIGN.md 4a), the 2600's Kelly covers 0.40 screen widths a second and
8933               	; 	' its Harry 0.193 -- a ratio of 2.07. At hsp4 = 6 ours was 2.67, and the
8934               	; 	' crook visibly strolled. 7 brings it to 2.29. 8 would match the original
8935               	; 	' exactly and cannot be used: Kelly's route here is 1.95x Harry's, so a
8936               	; 	' speed ratio of 2.0 leaves the on-foot chase 0.2 s of slack, which is no
8937               	; 	' chase at all. checkchase.py holds the line.
8938               	; 	' STILL RUNNING FROM THE STAIRS -- and this has to come BEFORE the move,
8939               	; 	' not after it. It sat below the two `IF hmv` blocks, where all it could
8940               	; 	' still reach was the animation counter: he faced the right way, played
8941               	; 	' the run cycle and did not travel a pixel. He stood at the foot of the
8942               	; 	' flight looking busy.
8943               	; 	'
8944               	; 	' TWO THINGS END THE RUN, AND BOTH ARE VISIBLE ON SCREEN.
8945               	; 	'
8946               	; 	' THE KOP ARRIVING ON THIS FLOOR OUTRANKS IT. This used to override the
8947               	; 	' flee as well as the climb, so for the whole of the run he held one
8948               	; 	' heading no matter where Kelly was -- and ran straight into him without
8949               	; 	' appearing to notice he was there. The flee above has already worked out
8950               	; 	' which way is away; clearing the run simply lets it stand.
8951               	; 	'
8952               	; 	' AND REACHING THE END WALL ENDS IT. It used to be a count of passes,
8953               	; 	' which expired somewhere in the middle of a floor and turned him round
8954               	; 	' for no reason the player could see. Running until the wall means every
8955               	; 	' change of direction has something on screen behind it.

9037               cv585
9038               	;
9039               	; 	' HE WALKS BY REAL TIME, NOT BY LOOP PASS -- one accumulate-and-drain per
9040               	; 	' ELAPSED FRAME rather than per pass. Everything that moves here used to be
9041               	; 	' per pass while the CLOCK is paced by the frame delta, so a busy screen
9042               	; 	' slowed Harry in real seconds and the clock did not slow with him: the
9043               	; 	' same uninterrupted escape finished with nine timer units in hand from a
9044               	; 	' light screen and none at all from the lift screen, decided by where the
9045               	; 	' player happened to be standing. Now he covers the same ground per second
9046               	; 	' wherever anybody is (DESIGN.md 0f-ter).
9047               	; 	'
9048               	; 	' A LOOP, NOT A MULTIPLY. `*` compiles to a real TMS9900 MPY, and reading a
9049               	; 	' 16-bit variable straight after one returns the product's HIGH word
9050               	; 	' (CLAUDE.md 3A). fdv is 1..3 here, so this is at most three adds.
9051               	; 	'
9052               	; 	' SIXTY-FOURTHS, AND ONLY TWO DRAINS -- both fall out of accumulating per
9053               	; 	' FRAME instead of per pass. He covers under a pixel a frame, so two drains
9054               	; 	' are ample where the per-pass version needed three; and the finer unit is
9055               	; 	' what makes the speed tunable at all. In sixteenths one step was about six
9056               	; 	' SECONDS of his route, so nothing landed near the buzzer; in sixty-fourths
9057               	; 	' it is about one and a half. Keep hsp64 <= 128: hacc is under 64 on entry,
9058               	; 	' so 64 + 128 stays inside a byte and two drains always suffice.
9059               	; 	'
9060               	; 	' AND THE STEP IS CLAMPED TO THREE FRAMES' WORTH, WHICH IS NOT A DETAIL.
9061               	; 	' Two tests sample his position once a pass and neither interpolates:
9062               	; 	'
9063               	; 	'   ARRIVAL   `hdx < 6` around the escalator or the roof door -- an ELEVEN
9064               	; 	'             pixel window. A step over 10 px can jump clean across it and
9065               	; 	'             he never arrives at all.
9066               	; 	'   THE CATCH `hdd < CATCHR` is 12, a 23-wide band, and Kelly closes 4 of
9067               	; 	'             it himself -- so Harry over ~18 px could pass through the Kop
9068               	; 	'             between two passes without either sample being inside it.
9069               	; 	'
9070               	; 	' FIVE FRAMES, AND THREE WAS TOO FEW. With two drains he moves at most 2 px
9071               	; 	' a frame, so five frames is 10 px -- and to skip the window he would have
9072               	; 	' to start outside it on one side and land outside it on the other, which
9073               	; 	' needs a step of 12. Ten is safe with a pixel to spare, and 10 + 4 = 14
9074               	; 	' against a 23-wide catch band.
9075               	; 	'
9076               	; 	' It was three, and three was the same bug in miniature. The lift screen
9077               	; 	' runs at almost exactly 20 passes a second, which IS fdv = 3, so every
9078               	; 	' momentary hitch there pushed past the clamp and he lost that frame --
9079               	; 	' measured as TIME 02 left on the lift screen against TIME 04 on a light
9080               	; 	' one, where before the real-time change it had been 00 against 09. The
9081               	; 	' clamp only exists for a genuine stall, and at five it only fires there.
9082               	; 	'
9083               	; 	' The ESCALATOR RIDE is deliberately untouched: it returns above this,
9084               	; 	' clocked one step per pass to stay locked to the moving staircase. A rider
9085               	; 	' advanced by the frame delta either drifts off the treads or makes the
9086               	; 	' steps run backwards (CLAUDE.md 3A, DESIGN.md 0f). It is about 3% of his
9087               	; 	' journey.
9088               	; 	'
9089               	; 	' `hanim` still advances by hspd and is still an ODOMETER -- pixels

9311               	; 	' hspd is 0 on a third of his frames. On every one of those the legs took
9312               	; 	' another step while the ground did not move, which is precisely what
9313               	; 	' scrubbing your feet looks like. It is the classic foot-slip: the legs
9314               	; 	' and the world running off two different clocks.

9575 DE9E C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0
9576 DEA0 0450  20 	b *r0
9577               	;
9578               	; 	' ======================================================================
9579               	; 	' COLLISIONS -- pure arithmetic against RAM, no VDP reads
9580               	; 	' ======================================================================
9581               	; 	' Kelly's box is [kfh, kfh + kh) above the slab; an obstacle's is
9582               	; 	' [ohb, oht). The ball's inset hitbox is what makes 8..10 avoidable both
9583               	; 	' ways -- without it there is a height at which the ball can be neither
9584               	; 	' jumped nor ducked, and an unavoidable hazard is not difficulty.
9585               	; 	' ONE HIT PER CONTACT, WITH A REFRACTORY PERIOD.
9586               	; 	'
9587               	; 	' The overlap test is true on every frame the player is touching
9588               	; 	' something, so with no latch at all one clumsy cart charges nine seconds
9589               	; 	' several times over -- and what the player sees is the clock jumping by
9590               	; 	' 27, from which the only available conclusion is that the penalty is
9591               	; 	' broken.
9592               	; 	'
9593               	; 	' A latch that clears on the first non-overlapping frame is still not
9594               	; 	' enough, and the case that breaks it is the BOUNCING BALL: it rises off
9595               	; 	' the player between bounces, which is a genuine loss of contact, so a
9596               	; 	' player standing still under one is taxed on every single bounce while
9597               	; 	' being given no opportunity to do anything about it. Technically two
9598               	; 	' collisions; in play, one situation.
9599               	; 	'
9600               	; 	' So obht() is a countdown, not a flag. A hit sets it to HITREF; it ticks
9601               	; 	' down only while the player is CLEAR, and the same obstacle cannot charge
9602               	; 	' again until it reaches zero. Contact holds the count up, so parking
9603               	; 	' inside something never earns a second penalty either.
9604               	; coll_obst:
9605               cvb_COLL_OBST
9606               	; 	IF klst = ST_ESC THEN RETURN		' riding is invincible, per the
9607 DEA2 D020  34 	movb @cvb_KLST,r0
     DEA4 21A2

     DEA8 0300

     DEB0 DEB6

9903               	; 	' THE FLOOR CLEARS WHEN YOU ARE HIT, and stays clear until you re-enter
9904               	; 	' the screen. Nine seconds is a heavy penalty on a fifty-unit clock, and
9905               	; 	' taking it while still standing among the things that charged it -- with
9906               	; 	' a second hazard 48 px behind the first -- is how one mistake becomes
9907               	; 	' three. Clearing the row makes the penalty a single event you can walk
9908               	; 	' away from.
9909               	; 	'
9910               	; 	' BOTH SLOTS, WHATEVER IS IN THEM. Radios used to be exempt: they are
9911               	; 	' CHARACTERS stamped into the name table rather than sprites, so zeroing
9912               	; 	' the kind would have stopped them colliding while leaving them plainly
9913               	; 	' visible on the shelf, and a fixture that is still there has to still be
9914               	; 	' there. That was a reason to UNDRAW them, not a reason to keep them --
9915               	; 	' and it left the one hazard that cannot leave on its own as the only one
9916               	; 	' the mercy did not cover. haz_off now takes the characters with it.
9917               	; 	'
9918               	; 	' NOTHING RESTORES IT HERE. load_band repopulates the band from the
9919               	; 	' template, and it runs only on a seam crossing or a round start, so
9920               	; 	' "until the screen is re-entered" costs no state and no timer: leave and
9921               	; 	' come back and the hazards are simply placed again.
9922               	; 	GOSUB haz_gone
9923 E122 06A0  32 	bl @jsr
     E124 A980

10137               	; 	' KELLY FLASHES WHILE HE IS KNOCKED ABOUT. Hitting an obstacle set a

10139               	; 	' screen said so -- the time simply went. A flash is the arcade's own
10140               	; 	' idiom for "that hit you", costs one variable, and is readable even
10141               	; 	' when the collision happened off the edge of the player's attention.

10165               	; 	' KELLY IS SPRITES 0, 1 AND 2 and nothing else ever is -- the VDP drops
10166               	; 	' the highest-numbered sprites on an over-full scanline, so the lowest
10167               	; 	' slots are the ones that can never disappear, and the player is the one
10168               	; 	' thing that must never disappear.
10169               	; 	'
10170               	; 	' THE BAND SPRITES SHARE A y ON PURPOSE. Slots 0 and 1 both sit at ky and
10171               	; 	' between them cover rows 0-15; slot 2 sits at ky+16. Boxes 0/1 and box 2
10172               	; 	' never share a scanline, so an actor costs at most TWO boxes on any line
10173               	; 	' -- and the VDP counts boxes, not pixels, so an empty overlap would have
10174               	; 	' cost just as much as a full one. Two actors meeting is four, exactly
10175               	; 	' the per-line limit, which is why obstacles are suppressed on Harry's
10176               	; 	' floor rather than merely being a kindness.
10177               	; 	ky = flry(klv)

10321               	; 		' FOUR RUN FRAMES FROM TWO BITS of the animation counter, and no
10322               	; 		' divide -- `/` compiles to a real TMS9900 DIV (CLAUDE.md 3A) and
10323               	; 		' this is per-frame code. Two adjacent bits give 0,4,8,12.
10324               	; 		'
10325               	; 		' KELLY USES BITS 2 AND 3 AND HARRY USES 3 AND 4, because a stride
10326               	; 		' has to cover about a stride's worth of ground or the figure
10327               	; 		' skates. Kelly runs 4 px a frame, so four frames a pose is 16 px;
10328               	; 		' Harry runs 1.75, so eight frames a pose is 14. Matching the two
10329               	; 		' rates to the two speeds is what keeps both looking like running.

10611               	; 	' HIS ARMS SWING, on the same bit of the animation counter that picks the
10612               	; 	' legs -- so the arm that is forward is the one opposite the leading leg,
10613               	; 	' which is what running looks like.
10614               	; 	' THE ARMS SWING ONCE PER FULL LEG CYCLE, ON BIT 16 -- not on bit 8.
10615               	; 	'
10616               	; 	' Bit 8 is one BEAT, and the legs take four of them (bits 8 and 16). So an
10617               	; 	' arm swing keyed to bit 8 completed twice for every one pass of the legs:
10618               	; 	' the top half of him flapping at double the speed of the bottom half.
10619               	; 	' That is the jig. It is not a drawing problem and no amount of redrawing
10620               	; 	' the legs would have touched it.
10621               	; 	'
10622               	; 	' On bit 16 the arms hold through beats 1-2 and swap for 3-4, which is
10623               	; 	' also the right PHASE: beat 1 leads with one leg and beat 3 with the
10624               	; 	' other, so the arm opposite the leading leg is forward in each -- which
10625               	; 	' is what running looks like.
10626               	; 	' THE TORSO RUNS ON THE SAME TWO BITS AS THE LEGS. With four drawn poses
10627               	; 	' the shoulders and arms swing WITH the stride; the old two-frame body had
10628               	; 	' a clock of its own, which is what read as a flap rather than a run.
10629               	; 	hp = P_HBODY

10705               	; 	' HE IS STRIPED FROM CAP TO HEM. Both stripe colours run the whole upper
10706               	; 	' half, so both boxes span rows 0-15 and neither can be tucked away --
10707               	; 	' affordable only because Kelly's hat moved into a box of its own. His
10708               	; 	' FACE is pushed DOWN instead (hy+3, box 3..18) so it clears the cap
10709               	; 	' rows, where his two stripe boxes plus Kelly's hat and face already
10710               	; 	' make four. Worst line of a meeting: exactly four, nothing dropped.
10711               	; 	' HE RISES A PIXEL ON THE RECOVERY BEATS. A runner is highest at mid-flight
10712               	; 	' and lowest at contact; with the body pinned at a constant y, alternating
10713               	; 	' legs under a motionless torso reads as a dance rather than as travel.
10714               	; 	'
10715               	; 	' One pixel is enough at this size, and it lifts the BODY ONLY -- the legs
10716               	; 	' keep their y, so the planted foot stays on the floor and the figure
10717               	; 	' stretches at the waist instead of hopping. Lifting the legs too would be
10718               	; 	' a jump.
10719               	; 	'
10720               	; 	' Beats 2 and 4 are the recoveries, which is exactly `hanim AND 8` -- the
10721               	; 	' cycle's LOW beat bit. IT MUST MOVE WITH THE BEAT BITS ABOVE IT: on the
10722               	; 	' wrong bit he rises and falls twice per stride, a second clock inside one
10723               	; 	' figure, which is the flapping of DESIGN.md 0j2 in the vertical.
10724               	; 	'
10725               	; 	' Safe because HARRY'S COLLISION HAS NO VERTICAL COMPONENT: he is caught on
10726               	; 	' `same level AND |dx| < CATCHR`. Doing this to Kelly would desynchronise
10727               	; 	' his sprite from the ball and biplane windows in DESIGN.md 5a, which is
10728               	; 	' why it is not done to him.
10729               	; 	' THE WHOLE FIGURE RISES, legs included, rather than the body stretching
10730               	; 	' away from the feet. Two reasons, and the second was not the first plan:
10731               	; 	'
10732               	; 	'   * It is what a run does. Mid-flight BOTH feet are off the ground, so
10733               	; 	'     lifting the legs with the body is the correct pose, not a compromise.
10734               	; 	'   * Lifting the body alone opens a one-pixel gap at the waist, and the
10735               	; 	'     torso's bottom row and the legs' top row do not align across it --
10736               	; 	'     green shows through and he reads as coming apart.
10737               	; 	'
10738               	; 	' It also keeps `hfy = hy - 7` and `hy2 = hy + 16` LITERAL, which is not a
10739               	; 	' cosmetic concern: assets/checkbands.py reads each band's draw offset by
10740               	; 	' parsing those exact expressions. Bobbing a differently-named variable
10741               	; 	' made them unreadable and the gate failed the build -- correctly. A
10742               	; 	' checker that can no longer see what it checks has to fail, not shrug.
10743               	; 	IF hanim AND 8 THEN hy = hy - 1

10927               	; 		' only while he is on THIS SCREEN. Once you are face to face the
10928               	; 		' round is a foot race you can see, and a ball arriving in the
10929               	; 		' middle of it costs nine seconds for reasons that have nothing to
10930               	; 		' do with the chase.
10931               	; 		'
10932               	; 		' THE SCREEN TEST IS WHAT MAKES IT INVISIBLE. Clearing his whole
10933               	; 		' floor regardless of screen meant the cleared band moved with him,
10934               	; 		' so every time he changed storeys one floor's hazards vanished and
10935               	; 		' another's appeared in the same instant -- and with all four bands
10936               	; 		' in view at once that reads as objects hopping between levels. Now
10937               	; 		' it only happens on the screen he is actually on, where the reason
10938               	; 		' for it is standing right there.
10939               	; 		'
10940               	; 		' Radios are unaffected: they are characters, drawn straight into
10941               	; 		' the name table, and a fixture the crook happens to run past is
10942               	; 		' not the thing this rule is about.
10943               	; 		' THE RADIO IS NOT A SPRITE ANY MORE -- it is four characters,
10944               	; 		' stamped by draw_radios after the band blit. Zeroing the local
10945               	; 		' copy hides both its slots; obk() is untouched, so coll_obst
10946               	; 		' still sees it and it still costs nine seconds.

11216               	; 	' Rows 21-23, 16 chars wide, centred: 48 characters whose PATTERNS are
11217               	; 	' poked directly, so a moving dot costs two writes and no name-table
11218               	; 	' traffic. With the flip (DESIGN.md 2a) this is how you know where Harry
11219               	; 	' is at all -- he can be one pixel off-screen and completely invisible.
11220               	; 	'
11221               	; 	' Rows 21-23 all sit in the THIRD screen third, so every scanner pattern
11222               	; 	' is at base 4096 and no third-selection arithmetic is needed.
11223               	; 	'
11224               	; 	' BOTH DOTS ARE WHITE and Kelly's BLINKS. The manual wants Kelly black
11225               	; 	' and Harry white, but this is the TMS9918's two-colours-per-8x1-cell
11226               	; 	' mode: a black dot and a white dot on the same pixel row of the same
11227               	; 	' character cannot both exist. Blinking distinguishes them with one
11228               	; 	' colour, which is what RallyX does with its player dot.
11229               	; 	' THE STEPS MOVE, AND EVERY ESCALATOR ON SCREEN MOVES WITH THEM. A flight
11230               	; 	' is twelve characters -- six per direction, sliced out of a rendered
11231               	; 	' 64x40 bitmap by genart.py -- so rewriting those twelve PATTERNS animates
11232               	; 	' the lot: 96 bytes, no name-table traffic, and the same cost whether one
11233               	; 	' flight is visible or ten. The phases slide the cells ALONG the slope, so
11234               	; 	' the steps travel up the flight rather than drifting sideways.
11235               	; esc_tick:
11236               cvb_ESC_TICK
11237               	; 	' ONLY SCREENS 0 AND 7 CARRY A FLIGHT, so six screens in eight skip this
11238               	; 	' entirely -- which is what makes rewriting the cells every frame
11239               	; 	' affordable. Nested, not `klsc > 0 AND klsc < 7`: the 9900 backend
11240               	; 	' miscompiles a compare-AND-compare (CLAUDE.md 3A).
11241               	; 	IF klsc > 0 THEN
11242 EB26 D020  34 	movb @cvb_KLSC,r0
     EB28 2190     
11243 EB2A 0201  20 	li r1,256   ; 1*256
     EB2C 0100     
11244 EB2E 9040  18 	cb r0,r1
11245 EB30 1402  14 	jhe cv808
11246 EB32 0460  28 	b @cv807
     EB34 EB4A     
11247               cv808
11248               	; 		IF klsc < 7 THEN RETURN
11249 EB36 D020  34 	movb @cvb_KLSC,r0
     EB38 2190     
11250 EB3A 0201  20 	li r1,1792   ; 7*256
     EB3C 0700     
11251 EB3E 9040  18 	cb r0,r1
11252 EB40 1A02  14 	jl cv810
11253 EB42 0460  28 	b @cv809
     EB44 EB4A     
11254               cv810
11255 EB46 C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0
11256 EB48 0450  20 	b *r0
11257               cv809
11258               	; 	END IF
11259               cv807
11260               	; 	' ONE PHASE PER PASS, AND NEVER MORE. There are four phases covering an
11261               	; 	' 8 px period, so advancing by the frame delta ALIASES: at fdv 2 the
11262               	; 	' pattern flips between two positions half a period apart and the
11263               	; 	' direction stops being readable, and at fdv 3 the sequence runs 0,3,2,1
11264               	; 	' -- the staircase visibly runs BACKWARDS, carrying its steps down while
11265               	; 	' the rider goes up. That is the wagon-wheel effect, and no amount of
11266               	; 	' phase arithmetic fixes it: a 4-phase cycle can only be stepped by 1.
11267               	; 	'
11268               	; 	' So the ANIMATION is the clock here, and the rider is paced from it --
11269               	; 	' see move_kelly, which advances him by the same fixed 2-and-1 per pass
11270               	; 	' rather than by fdv. Locking the two together is the only way they
11271               	; 	' cannot drift, and it is what "the player moves at the rate the steps
11272               	; 	' carry him" actually means. The cost is that a ride takes 36 passes
11273               	; 	' rather than 36 frames, so it slows down when the loop does -- which is
11274               	; 	' the honest behaviour for a machine that is carrying you.
11275               	; 	escp = escp + 1
11276 EB4A D020  34 	movb @cvb_ESCP,r0
     EB4C 20B1     
11277 EB4E 0220  22 	ai r0,256	; 1
     EB50 0100

     EB54 20B1

     EB58 0400

     EB60 EB68

     EB66 20B1     
11287               cv811
11288               	; 	' ONLY THE CELLS THAT MOVE, AND ONLY THIS SCREEN'S. genart.py measures
11289               	; 	' which cells move -- the step cells and the COMPOSITE copies of them
11290               	; 	' that cross a floor -- and groups them WEST then EAST, because only one
11291               	; 	' direction is ever on screen: screen 0 carries a west flight and screen
11292               	; 	' 7 an east one.
11293               	; 	'
11294               	; 	' THIS IS THE ONLY PER-PASS WORK UNIQUE TO THESE TWO SCREENS, and it is
11295               	; 	' `define_char`, which in bitmap mode is LDIRVM3 -- the TRIPLE copy, once
11296               	; 	' per screen third. It therefore costs three times what the character
11297               	; 	' count suggests, which is why these screens run slower than the rest.
11298               	; 	'
11299               	; 	' THE WEST SCREEN USED TO COST HALF AS MUCH AGAIN, because it carries TWO
11300               	; 	' flights: floor 1's crosses into a shop floor and floor 3's into the
11301               	; 	' roof, so it needed both composite sets -- nine characters against the
11302               	; 	' east's six, 216 bytes a pass against 144. It measured 20 passes a
11303               	; 	' second where the east screen managed 24-26. The two sets are
11304               	; 	' PIXEL-IDENTICAL and are never wanted in the same screen THIRD, so they
11305               	; 	' share their characters now and are told apart by the per-third colour
11306               	; 	' table (see esc_deck_col in setup). Six either side.
11307               	; 	'
11308               	; 	' The handrail and the frame sit past the end of both ranges and never
11309               	; 	' move at all.

11571               	;
11572               	; 	' ------------------------------------------------- the scanner's furniture
11573               	; 	' Without this the scanner is two dots in a void: you can see THAT Harry is
11574               	; 	' somewhere, but not what he is near, which is the one thing you actually
11575               	; 	' need when he is off-screen. Floor lines say which level, the slashes say
11576               	; 	' where that floor's escalator is, and the bar says where the elevator is --
11577               	; 	' exactly the three things the manual lists.
11578               	; 	'
11579               	; 	' FURNITURE AND DOTS ARE SEGREGATED BY PIXEL ROW ON PURPOSE, and a level
11580               	; 	' band is now FOUR rows, one per colour, because a pixel row carries
11581               	; 	' exactly one colour here:
11582               	; 	'
11583               	; 	'   +0  GREY    escalator head, and the elevator car
11584               	; 	'   +1  GREY    the air of that floor
11585               	; 	'   +2  GREY
11586               	; 	'   +3  YELLOW  the floor line -- and NOTHING else goes here
11587               	; 	'
11588               	; 	' THE FIRST THREE ROWS ARE THE FLOOR'S AIR AND EVERYTHING LIVES IN THEM:
11589               	; 	' the escalator's three-step diagonal, the lift car, and both actors, all
11590               	; 	' three pixels tall. They are grey in the table, and the Kop and the
11591               	; 	' crook recolour their own CHARACTER as they move (scan_mark) -- which is
11592               	; 	' what lets both of them fill the band instead of getting a pixel row
11593               	; 	' each. Eight pixels of this canvas is half a screen of the store, so
11594               	; 	' they are almost never in one character; when they are, they merge.
11595               	; 	'
11596               	; 	' Shrinking from six rows bought eight empty pixel rows, four above the
11597               	; 	' instrument and four below, so it no longer butts against the shop floor
11598               	; 	' above it or the bottom of the screen.
11599               	; 	'
11600               	; 	' The dots are erased by ANDing their bits out, so anything sharing a row
11601               	; 	' with one is rubbed away wherever an actor has passed -- which is why
11602               	; 	' scan_escs and scan_elev redraw every tick rather than once.

11876               	;
11877               	; 	' THE FLIGHT IS BLACK, and it has to be written per character because the
11878               	; 	' colour table only knows rows -- the same reason the two actors carry
11879               	; 	' their own colour (scan_mark).
11880               	; 	'
11881               	; 	' This runs BEFORE the markers every tick, which is the whole ordering
11882               	; 	' the radar needs: the lift car is painted first and the flights next, so
11883               	; 	' a Kop or a crook standing on either of them paints over it. Nothing
11884               	; 	' moves aside to make room -- the pixels are simply ORed together and the
11885               	; 	' last colour written wins, so an actor in the lift shaft shows as an
11886               	; 	' actor rather than shifting anywhere.

12020 F0FA C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0
12021 F0FC 0450  20 	b *r0
12022               	;
12023               	; 	' Blank the canvas by WRITING zeros rather than copying 384 of them out
12024               	; 	' of ROM. This runs twice a game -- at setup and on a new Krook -- so a
12025               	; 	' loop costs nothing anybody can perceive, and it hands the fixed area
12026               	; 	' back the best part of half a kilobyte, which is the budget that
12027               	; 	' actually binds. Paced in bursts: a few hundred VDP writes in a single
12028               	; 	' frame are silently dropped (CLAUDE.md 3A).
12029               	; scan_wipe:
12030               cvb_SCAN_WIPE
12031               	; 	' THIS ADDRESS IS A CHARACTER CODE IN DISGUISE, AND IT WENT STALE.
12032               	; 	' 4096 is the pattern table's BOTTOM THIRD -- rows 16-23, which is
12033               	; 	' band 0 and the radar together -- and the canvas characters begin at
12034               	; 	' genart's SCAN_FIRST, so the base is 4096 + SCAN_FIRST*8. It was
12035               	; 	' written out by hand as 4096 + 160*8 when SCAN_FIRST was 160; art
12036               	; 	' added later moved SCAN_FIRST to 208 and nothing moved this with it,
12037               	; 	' so the wipe zeroed the 48 characters BELOW the canvas and never
12038               	; 	' touched the canvas at all.
12039               	; 	'
12040               	; 	' Two of those forty-eight are STRUCTURE. ENDWALL is the building's
12041               	; 	' outside wall and SLABP is a pillar's cap. Blanking a pattern in ONE
12042               	; 	' THIRD leaves a character that draws correctly everywhere else and
12043               	; 	' falls back to its own background colour here -- so the GROUND FLOOR's
12044               	; 	' outside wall turned into plain green while the identical wall one
12045               	; 	' storey up was perfect, and the ground floor's pillars lost the brick
12046               	; 	' carried up through the bar above them. ENDWALL appears only on
12047               	; 	' screens 0 and 7, which is exactly why it was reported as happening
12048               	; 	' only on the escalator screens, mirrored side to side.
12049               	; 	'
12050               	; 	' The name table was correct the whole time. Probing it said so, four
12051               	; 	' times. It was the GLYPH that was empty, in one third only -- which a
12052               	; 	' probe that copies the character somewhere else to look at it cannot
12053               	; 	' see, because the copy renders from a different third's pattern.
12054               	; 	'
12055               	; 	' renumber.py rewrites this line from genart.SCAN_FIRST, and
12056               	; 	' checkstruct.py fails the build if the two disagree.
12057               	; 	#swa = 5760			' 4096 + SCAN_FIRST*8, the canvas patterns
12058 F0FE 0200  20 	li r0,5760
     F100 1680

     F104 20E0

12128               	;
12129               	; 	' THE TWO ACTORS ARE SPRITES; EVERYTHING ELSE IS CHARACTERS.
12130               	; 	'
12131               	; 	' That split is what makes the instrument simple. The floor lines, the
12132               	; 	' flights and the lift car do not move relative to the canvas, so they
12133               	; 	' are pixels in the scanner's own characters and cost nothing to keep.
12134               	; 	' The Kop and the crook DO move, and as sprites they need no colour
12135               	; 	' table, no erase and no priority arithmetic -- they draw over the
12136               	; 	' furniture because sprites always do, and nothing shifts to make room.
12137               	; 	'
12138               	; 	' IT ALSO REMOVED THE BLINK. Kelly flashed because a pixel row of this
12139               	; 	' canvas can only carry one colour, so at close range the two markers
12140               	; 	' merged and the blink was the only thing left to tell them apart. Two
12141               	; 	' sprites are two colours wherever they stand.
12142               	; 	'
12143               	; 	' The furniture is drawn ONCE, by scan_furn. It used to be redrawn every
12144               	; 	' tick because erasing a character-based marker ANDed bits out of
12145               	; 	' whatever it was standing on; there is nothing left to rub it away. The
12146               	; 	' car still redraws itself, because the car actually moves.
12147               	; 	' -- the lift car. IT WAS FLICKERING, and the cause was not the drawing
12148               	; 	' but the ERASING: scan_elev cleared its three cells and put them back
12149               	; 	' every tick, whether or not the car had moved, so several times a second
12150               	; 	' there was a window with the pixels off. A sprite has no erase at all --
12151               	; 	' it is placed, and the VDP does the rest -- and slot 26 sits BELOW the
12152               	; 	' two markers, so the Kop and the crook pass over it without either of
12153               	; 	' them having to know the car exists.

12866               	;
12867               	; 	' THE BONUS IS COUNTED OUT, NOT AWARDED. Dropping the whole sum into the
12868               	; 	' score in one frame tells the player a number; ticking the clock down a
12869               	; 	' unit at a time, with the score climbing and a beat under each step,
12870               	; 	' tells them what the number was FOR. It is the pay-off for every second
12871               	; 	' they saved, and every arcade game of this era spends a moment on it.
12872               	; 	'
12873               	; 	' It also costs nothing to get right: the loop already has to run down
12874               	; 	' `tsec`, so the animation IS the calculation, and the repeated-addition
12875               	; 	' note below stops applying -- each step adds one band's worth.
12876               	; 	'
12877               	; 	' x100 for Krooks 1-9, x200 for 10-15, x300 from 16. Two independent
12878               	; 	' sources agree on those bands; the manual's own wording suggests
12879               	; 	' 1-8 / 9-16 / 17+ and DESIGN.md 0 records the disagreement.
12880               	; 	'
12881               	; 	' #bval MUST BE 16-BIT: 300 does not fit in a byte, and a plain variable
12882               	; 	' would silently truncate it to 44 (CLAUDE.md 3A).
12883               	; bonus_count:
12884               cvb_BONUS_COUNT

12985 F84C 0460  28 	b @cvb_BN_LOOP
     F84E F7A0     
12986               	;
12987               	; 	' `calc_bonus` USED TO LIVE HERE and computed the whole sum by repeated
12988               	; 	' addition, to dodge two hazards at once: `tsec * bmul` is an 8-bit
12989               	; 	' product (a 50-unit capture in the x300 band pays 15,000, six times past
12990               	; 	' a byte), and multiplying properly lands on the MPY/r0 hazard where a
12991               	; 	' 16-bit variable read straight after being multiplied returns the
12992               	; 	' product's high word. Counting the bonus out a unit at a time (above)
12993               	; 	' removes the multiply entirely rather than working around it -- the loop
12994               	; 	' the player watches IS the arithmetic.
12995               	; 	'
12996               	; 	' It also fixed the last-tick case for free: `bn_loop` tests `tsec = 0`
12997               	; 	' on entry, where the old `FOR bi = 1 TO tsec` would have run its body
12998               	; 	' once and paid a full multiplier for no time at all (CLAUDE.md 3A).
12999               	;
13000               	; do_escape:
13001               cvb_DO_ESCAPE
13002               	; 	escapd = 0
13003 F850 04C0  14 	clr r0
13004 F852 D800  38 	movb r0,@cvb_ESCAPD
     F854 210A     
13005               	; 	rsn = 0
13006 F856 04C0  14 	clr r0
13007 F858 D800  38 	movb r0,@cvb_RSN
     F85A 21FE     
13008               	; 	GOTO lose_kop
13009 F85C 0460  28 	b @cvb_LOSE_KOP
     F85E F88C     
13010               	;
13011               	; 	' Two ways to lose a Kop here, and the message has to be read BEFORE the
13012               	; 	' flags are cleared -- clearing first made the biplane test dead code and
13013               	; 	' every biplane death say TIME UP.
13014               	; do_death:
13015               cvb_DO_DEATH
13016               	; 	rsn = 2

     F862 0200     
13018 F864 D800  38 	movb r0,@cvb_RSN
     F866 21FE

13020 F868 D020  34 	movb @cvb_DEAD,r0
     F86A 20FB     
13021 F86C 0201  20 	li r1,256   ; 1*256
     F86E 0100

     F876 F880

13040               	; 	' THE MESSAGE BOX, ON THE GAME SCREEN AND NOT INSTEAD OF IT.
13041               	; 	'
13042               	; 	' It used to CLS before GAME OVER, so the last thing the player saw was
13043               	; 	' the store being deleted and then two words on an empty field -- which
13044               	; 	' reads as the program ending rather than as the game ending. The store
13045               	; 	' stays; the message sits in the middle of it.
13046               	; 	'
13047               	; 	' THE BOX IS THE FONT'S OWN BACKGROUND. Every font character is black on
13048               	; 	' HUD_BG (dark blue), so a row of SPACES is a solid dark blue bar. A blank
13049               	; 	' row above and below the text is a frame for the price of two strings and
13050               	; 	' no new characters, and it reads against the store because nothing else on
13051               	; 	' the playfield is that colour.
13052               	; 	'
13053               	; 	' THIRTEEN WIDE, WHICH IS THE LONGEST MESSAGE PLUS ONE EITHER SIDE.
13054               	; 	' HE GOT AWAY and THE BIPLANE are both eleven. Every string below is padded
13055               	; 	' to exactly thirteen so the box has straight edges, and column 10 puts it
13056               	; 	' at columns 10-22 -- centre 16, the screen's.
13057               	; 	'
13058               	; 	' THE REASON IS ALWAYS ON THE SAME ROW, and that is what lets one layout
13059               	; 	' serve both cases. Losing a life draws three rows around it; losing the
13060               	; 	' last one draws two more ON TOP, so GAME OVER appears above the reason
13061               	; 	' rather than replacing it. Printing the reason at a different row for each
13062               	; 	' case would need `PRINT AT` with a variable, and every other PRINT in this
13063               	; 	' program uses a constant.

13227               	;
13228               	; 	' Silence every channel FIRST. SOUND latches, and the per-channel decay
13229               	; 	' counters are not ticked inside this loop -- a tone still sounding when
13230               	; 	' the pause starts would hang for its whole duration, which is the classic
13231               	; 	' CVBasic sticky-audio failure.
13232               	; 	' EVERY CHANNEL OFF, AND THE DECAY COUNTERS WITH THEM. The counters are
13233               	; 	' ticked by the main loop, so anything still sounding when the loop stops
13234               	; 	' -- for a capture, a death, the bonus count -- sustains for as long as
13235               	; 	' whatever replaced the loop takes. That is where the long beep over the
13236               	; 	' bonus tally came from: not the tally's own note, which turns itself off
13237               	; 	' after every unit, but a footstep or a hit that was still ringing when
13238               	; 	' Harry was caught.

13692 FDFE C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0
13693 FE00 0450  20 	b *r0
13694               	;
13695               	; 	' EVERYTHING BELOW THIS LINE IS ASSEMBLED INTO BANK 1, so the INCLUDE order
13696               	; 	' is load-bearing and nothing but data may follow it.
13697               	; 	'
13698               	; 	' THE FONT IS NOW IN THE BANK TOO, and it used to be deliberately outside:
13699               	; 	' keeping one readable thing in bank 0 means a bank-selection mistake shows
13700               	; 	' as "text survives, art does not" rather than a uniformly blank screen,
13701               	; 	' which is a genuinely useful thing to have when a bank goes wrong.
13702               	; 	'
13703               	; 	' It was given up for 472 bytes, because the fixed area had 342 left and
13704               	; 	' that diagnostic is worth less than the ability to keep building. Two
13705               	; 	' things make it a fair trade rather than a straight loss:
13706               	; 	'
13707               	; 	'   * `BANK SELECT 1` already runs BEFORE `DEFINE CHAR 32,59,font_bits`
13708               	; 	'     (see setup), so nothing about the read order had to change. If that
13709               	; 	'     ever stops being true the font goes blank at boot, immediately and
13710               	; 	'     unmistakably -- a loud failure, not a subtle one.
13711               	; 	'   * The alternative on the table was banking the 29 PRINT AT literals,
13712               	; 	'     which recovers LESS (about 360 bytes net after the reader routine)
13713               	; 	'     and would blind assets/checklayout.py, whose whole method is parsing
13714               	; 	'     `PRINT AT n,"literal"` to catch a string running past column 31 or
13715               	; 	'     a HUD poke landing inside a label. Trading a diagnostic for bytes is
13716               	; 	'     one thing; trading a build gate for fewer bytes is another.
13717               	; 	#if TI994A
13718               	; 	BANK 1
13719      01FC     BANK_0_FREE:	EQU >fffe-$
     **** ****     > .rept

13857               	; 	INCLUDE "art.bas"
13858               	; 	' Keystone Kapers art -- GENERATED by assets/genart.py. Do not edit; regenerate.
13859               	; 	'
13860               	; 	' EVERY SPRITE IS 16x16: sprite size and magnification are GLOBAL on the
13861               	; 	' TMS9918, so small objects are drawn small INSIDE a 16x16 box rather than
13862               	; 	' being small sprites.
13863               	; 	'
13864               	; 	' EVERY 8-PX OBJECT SITS IN ROWS 8-15 OF ITS BOX, ducked Kelly included, so
13865               	; 	' one rule places them all: bottom edge = y + 16, and a thing resting on the
13866               	; 	' floor is at y = FLOORY - 16.
13867               	; 	'
13868               	; 	' Sprite bytes are QUADRANT-ORDERED: 16 rows of the left half, then 16 of
13869               	; 	' the right. Every block is an EVEN number of bytes -- an odd DATA BYTE run
13870               	; 	' silently misaligns every word table after it (CLAUDE.md 3A).
13871               	;
13872               	; spr_kelly:	' Kelly: RIGHT hat/face/tunic/tunic-arm-up/duck-hat/duck-face, then the same LEFT (+24), then the FOUR shared run frames. Patterns 0..60
13873               cvb_SPR_KELLY
13874               	; 	DATA BYTE $00,$00,$00,$00,$00,$00,$00,$00

14869               	;
14870               	; 	' -------------------------------------------------- scanner canvas
14871               	; 	' Chars 208-255: 16 cols x 3 rows over screen rows 21-23. Blank to start;
14872               	; 	' dots are plotted by VPOKEing the PATTERN table (rows 21-23 sit in the
14873               	; 	' third screen third, so every scanner pattern is at base 4096).
14874               	;
14875               	; 	' --------------------------------------------------- font colours
14876               	; 	' Chars 32-90, EIGHT bytes each. Without this the font keeps whatever
14877               	; 	' CVBasic left in the colour table, which on a green store made the HUD
14878               	; 	' unreadable.
14879               	;
14880               	; scan_col3:	' one 8-byte block per canvas row: 4 px of GREY margin, then a 3 px band of furniture GREY and a yellow floor line, per 4 px level, on dark green. The Kop and the crook are coloured PER CHARACTER at run time -- see scan_set
14881               cvb_SCAN_COL3
14882               	; 	DATA BYTE $EE,$EE,$EE,$EE,$EC,$EC,$EC,$BC
14883 70B2 EEEE     	byte >ee,>ee,>ee,>ee,>ec,>ec,>ec,>bc
     70B4 EEEE     
     70B6 ECEC     
     70B8 ECBC     
14884               	; 	DATA BYTE $EC,$EC,$EC,$BC,$EC,$EC,$EC,$BC
14885 70BA ECEC     	byte >ec,>ec,>ec,>bc,>ec,>ec,>ec,>bc
     70BC ECBC     
     70BE ECEC     
     70C0 ECBC     
14886               	; 	DATA BYTE $EC,$EC,$EC,$BC,$EE,$EE,$EE,$EE
14887 70C2 ECEC     	byte >ec,>ec,>ec,>bc,>ee,>ee,>ee,>ee
     70C4 ECBC     
     70C6 EEEE     
     70C8 EEEE

14992               	; 	INCLUDE "store.bas"
14993               	; 	' Keystone Kapers store -- GENERATED by assets/genstore.py. Do not edit.
14994               	; 	'
14995               	; 	' EIGHT band templates of 5 rows x 32 cols (160 bytes each) plus a 32-byte
14996               	; 	' index over (level, screen). The whole 8-screen, 4-level building is about
14997               	; 	' 1.3 KB instead of the 5,120 bytes it would cost to store literally.
14998               	; 	'
14999               	; 	' All eight templates are ONE contiguous block under ONE label, so a band
15000               	; 	' blit is
15001               	; 	'     SCREEN stor_tpl, tpl*160, toprow*32, 32, 5, 32
15002               	; 	' with no label ladder -- SCREEN needs a literal label and a label cannot be
15003               	; 	' chosen by a variable, which is why RallyX needed a 4-way IF over its maze
15004               	; 	' tables. One block sidesteps it.
15005               	; 	'
15006               	; 	' 160 is EVEN, so this cannot leave the location counter odd and misalign
15007               	; 	' the word tables defined after it.
15008               	; 	'
15009               	; 	' template ids:
15010               	; 	'   0 T_AISLE_A
15011               	; 	'   1 T_AISLE_B
15012               	; 	'   2 T_ESC_W
15013               	; 	'   3 T_ESC_E
15014               	; 	'   4 T_ELEV
15015               	; 	'   5 T_ROOF0
15016               	; 	'   6 T_ROOF1
15017               	; 	'   7 T_ROOF2
15018               	; 	'   8 T_ROOF3
15019               	; 	'   9 T_ROOF4
15020               	; 	'   10 T_ROOF5
15021               	; 	'   11 T_ROOF6
15022               	; 	'   12 T_ROOF7
15023               	; 	'   13 T_END_W
15024               	; 	'   14 T_END_E
15025               	;
15026               	; stor_tpl:	' 15 templates x 160 bytes
15027               cvb_STOR_TPL
